# NDEB AFK Prep Pro - Full Question Bank


## Topic: ANATOMY

**Q1: Which cranial nerve provides motor innervation to the muscles of mastication?**

- A) CN V (Trigeminal) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) CN VII (Facial) 
- C) CN IX (Glossopharyngeal) 
- D) CN XII (Hypoglossal) 

*Explanation:* Great attempt! The trigeminal nerve (CN V) is absolutely correct because its mandibular division (V3) uniquely provides all motor innervation to the muscles of mastication, like the temporalis and masseter, essential for chewing. Other cranial nerves serve different primary motor functions, such as facial expression or tongue movement, but do not control the mechanics of mastication. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q2: The Stensen's duct drains which salivary gland?**

- A) Sublingual gland 
- B) Submandibular gland 
- C) Parotid gland ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Von Ebner's glands 

*Explanation:* No worries, this is a common point of distinction! The Stensen's duct is the excretory duct *specifically* for the parotid gland, emptying into the oral cavity opposite the maxillary second molar. Other major salivary glands, such as the submandibular and sublingual glands, have their own distinct duct systems (Wharton's duct and Bartholin's/ducts of Rivinus, respectively). Keep up the excellent work in clarifying these anatomical details!

---

**Q3: Which of the following structures passes through the foramen ovale?**

- A) Middle meningeal artery 
- B) Mandibular nerve (V3) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Maxillary nerve (V2) 
- D) Ophthalmic nerve (V1) 

*Explanation:* Great try! The Mandibular nerve (V3) is the correct answer because it's the third division of the trigeminal nerve (CN V) that exits the middle cranial fossa through the foramen ovale. Understanding the specific structures that pass through each foramen is crucial, as other cranial nerves and major vessels utilize different, distinct openings in the skull. Keep reviewing those cranial nerve pathways – you're doing great!

---

**Q4: How many deciduous teeth are in a complete primary dentition?**

- A) 20 ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) 24 
- C) 28 
- D) 32 

*Explanation:* That's a common point of confusion, but you're almost there! A complete primary dentition consists of **20** deciduous teeth: 8 incisors, 4 canines, and 8 molars. Unlike the permanent dentition, there are no premolars or third molars in the primary set, making 20 the definitive number. Keep up the great work studying the unique characteristics of primary dentition!

---

**Q5: The bulk of the tooth is made up of what substance?**

- A) Enamel 
- B) Dentin ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Pulp 
- D) Cementum 

*Explanation:* No worries, that's a common point of confusion for students! Dentin is the correct answer because it forms the primary bulk and main supporting structure of the tooth, underlying the enamel in the crown and the cementum in the root. While enamel and cementum are important outer layers and pulp is vital, they make up a much smaller proportion of the tooth's overall volume compared to dentin. Keep up the great work studying!

---

**Q6: Which muscle is responsible for smiling?**

- A) Buccinator 
- B) Orbicularis oris 
- C) Zygomaticus major ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Masseter 

*Explanation:* You're doing great! The Zygomaticus major is indeed the primary muscle responsible for a genuine, full smile because it originates from the zygomatic bone (cheekbone) and pulls the corner of the mouth superiorly and laterally. While other facial muscles create different expressions or assist in lip movements, this specific muscle's action is unique in producing that characteristic upward and outward pull. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q7: What is the largest salivary gland?**

- A) Sublingual 
- B) Submandibular 
- C) Parotid ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Von Ebner's 

*Explanation:* You're on the right track! The **parotid gland** is indeed the largest of the major salivary glands, contributing significantly to serous saliva production. While the submandibular and sublingual glands are also crucial, they are considerably smaller in size compared to the parotid. Keep up the great work; recognizing these anatomical distinctions is key!

---

**Q8: The lower jawbone is called the:**

- A) Maxilla 
- B) Mandible ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Zygoma 
- D) Sphenoid 

*Explanation:* That's a great effort! The correct anatomical term for the lower jawbone is indeed the **mandible**, which is the largest and strongest bone of the face and the only movable bone of the skull. Other options typically refer to different bones like the upper jaw (maxilla), the cheekbone (zygoma), or other cranial structures. Keep up the excellent work mastering these foundational terms for your exam!

---

**Q9: Which teeth are primarily used for grinding food?**

- A) Incisors 
- B) Canines 
- C) Premolars 
- D) Molars ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* Great effort! Molars are the correct answer because their broad, flat chewing surfaces and multiple cusps are specifically designed for crushing and grinding food into smaller, digestible pieces at the back of the mouth. Incisors are for biting, canines for tearing, and while premolars also help with grinding, molars are the primary teeth responsible for this heavy-duty function. Keep up the great work!

---

**Q10: The roof of the mouth is called the:**

- A) Gingiva 
- B) Palate ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Uvula 
- D) Tonsil 

*Explanation:* Great try! The correct answer is **Palate** because it is the precise anatomical term for the entire roof of the mouth, encompassing both the hard (bony) and soft (muscular) regions. Any other options would describe different oral structures or only a part of the palate itself, not the complete roof.

---

**Q11: Which part of the tooth is visible in the oral cavity?**

- A) Root 
- B) Crown ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Pulp 
- D) Cementum 

*Explanation:* Great effort on the question! The **crown** is the anatomical portion of the tooth normally visible above the gingiva in the oral cavity, covered by enamel. In contrast, the root is embedded within the alveolar bone, and the pulp and dentin are internal structures, none of which are typically visible in a healthy mouth. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q12: The facial nerve is which cranial nerve?**

- A) CN V 
- B) CN VII ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) CN VIII 
- D) CN IX 

*Explanation:* No worries, this is a common one to review! The **facial nerve is indeed Cranial Nerve VII (CN VII)**. Remembering the numerical sequence is crucial because each cranial nerve number, such as CN V for the trigeminal or CN X for the vagus nerve, uniquely identifies a distinct nerve with specific functions, meaning other numbers would correspond to different nerves. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q13: What is the anatomical term for the gums?**

- A) Mucosa 
- B) Alveolus 
- C) Gingiva ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Periodontium 

*Explanation:* You're doing great! The correct anatomical term for the gums is **Gingiva**. This is the precise medical term for the specialized masticatory mucosa surrounding the teeth, while other options likely refer to broader areas of the oral cavity or different supporting structures of the tooth. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q14: Which artery is the primary blood supply to the mandible?**

- A) Facial artery 
- B) Maxillary artery 
- C) Inferior alveolar artery ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Lingual artery 

*Explanation:* No worries, this is a common area to review! The Inferior Alveolar Artery is the primary blood supply because it's a direct branch of the Maxillary artery that specifically enters the mandible via the mandibular foramen and courses *within* the bone, supplying the mandibular teeth, bone, and surrounding structures directly. Other arteries in the region, while important, primarily supply the surrounding muscles, soft tissues, or are higher up in the arterial tree, not directly perfusing the mandibular bone itself like the Inferior Alveolar Artery. Keep up the great studying!

---

**Q15: The temporomandibular joint (TMJ) is a articulation between the mandible and which bone?**

- A) Sphenoid bone 
- B) Zygomatic bone 
- C) Temporal bone ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Parietal bone 

*Explanation:* Great effort on the question! The correct answer is indeed the **temporal bone**.

The temporomandibular joint, as its name suggests ("temporo" referring to the temporal bone and "mandibular" to the mandible), is formed by the articulation of the mandibular condyle with the mandibular fossa and articular eminence of the temporal bone. Other bones, while nearby, do not directly form the articulating surfaces of this unique synovial joint.

---

**Q16: How many roots does a typical maxillary first molar have?**

- A) One 
- B) Two 
- C) Three ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Four 

*Explanation:* That's a common area where students get confused, but you're on the right track! A typical maxillary first molar characteristically has **three roots**: two buccal (mesiobuccal and distobuccal) and one palatal. This unique trifurcation pattern helps distinguish it from other teeth, making "three" the definitive answer for this crucial tooth.

---

**Q17: What structure separates the thoracic cavity from the abdominal cavity?**

- A) Rib cage 
- B) Sternum 
- C) Diaphragm ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Pleura 

*Explanation:* You're on the right track! The **Diaphragm** is indeed the correct answer because it is the dome-shaped primary muscle of respiration that forms the floor of the thoracic cavity and the roof of the abdominal cavity, unequivocally separating them. Other structures, like organs, reside within these cavities but don't define the boundary between them. Keep up the excellent work – understanding these fundamental anatomical landmarks is crucial for your exam!

---

**Q18: Which part of the pharynx is located behind the oral cavity?**

- A) Nasopharynx 
- B) Oropharynx ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Laryngopharynx 
- D) Hypopharynx 

*Explanation:* No worries at all, that's a common point of distinction! The **Oropharynx** is indeed the correct answer because its anterior boundary is the oropharyngeal isthmus, placing it precisely behind the oral cavity. The nasopharynx is superior, located behind the nasal cavity, and the laryngopharynx (hypopharynx) is inferior, behind the larynx, not the oral cavity. Keep up the excellent work distinguishing these important anatomical regions!

---

**Q19: The lingual nerve provides general sensation to which part of the tongue?**

- A) Anterior two-thirds ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Posterior one-third 
- C) The entire tongue 
- D) The base of the tongue 

*Explanation:* That's a great question to review for the AFK, and you're doing well by identifying areas to strengthen! The lingual nerve specifically carries general sensation (touch, pain, temperature) from the **anterior two-thirds** of the tongue. General sensation for the posterior one-third is primarily supplied by the glossopharyngeal nerve, so any other option would be incorrect for the lingual nerve's role. Keep up the fantastic work!

---

**Q20: Which foramen does the maxillary division of the trigeminal nerve (V2) pass through?**

- A) Foramen spinosum 
- B) Foramen rotundum ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Foramen ovale 
- D) Jugular foramen 

*Explanation:* Great effort! The maxillary division of the trigeminal nerve (V2) exclusively exits the middle cranial fossa through the **foramen rotundum** to reach the pterygopalatine fossa. This is distinct from the ophthalmic division (V1) which uses the superior orbital fissure, and the mandibular division (V3) which passes through the foramen ovale. Keep up the excellent work in mastering this important anatomy!

---

**Q21: The mental foramen is located on which bone?**

- A) Maxilla 
- B) Mandible ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Palatine bone 
- D) Zygomatic bone 

*Explanation:* You're getting there! The mental foramen is a key landmark on the **mandible**, specifically on its outer surface, through which the mental nerve and vessels exit. It's exclusively found on the lower jaw, unlike the maxilla, temporal, or sphenoid bones which have their own distinct foramina. Keep up the great work!

---

**Q22: What is the name of the cusp on a maxillary first molar that is not always present?**

- A) Cusp of Carabelli ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Talon cusp 
- C) Dens evaginatus 
- D) Distal cusp 

*Explanation:* You're on the right track in reviewing this! The Cusp of Carabelli is indeed the correct answer because it's an accessory cusp found on the mesiolingual surface of maxillary first molars, known for its variability in presence and size—it's not always developed. The other primary cusps of the maxillary first molar, such as the mesiolingual, distolingual, mesiobuccal, and distobuccal cusps, are consistently present and fundamental to its anatomy. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q23: The pterygomandibular raphe is a landmark for which injection?**

- A) Gow-Gates mandibular block 
- B) Vazirani-Akinosi block 
- C) Inferior alveolar nerve block ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Mental nerve block 

*Explanation:* You're on the right track with understanding important anatomical landmarks! The pterygomandibular raphe is indeed a critical landmark for the Inferior Alveolar Nerve Block (IANB). This fibrous band helps define the medial border of the pterygomandibular space, where the inferior alveolar nerve is situated, making it a key guide for accurate needle insertion into this area. Other injections, such as the buccal, mental, or posterior superior alveolar nerve blocks, target different anatomical locations and rely on distinct landmarks for successful anesthesia.

---

**Q24: Which muscle forms the floor of the mouth?**

- A) Genioglossus 
- B) Mylohyoid ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Digastric 
- D) Stylohyoid 

*Explanation:* Great effort on that question!

The **Mylohyoid** muscle is indeed the correct answer because its bilateral sheets originate from the inner surface of the mandible and meet in the midline, creating a muscular diaphragm that forms the structural floor of the oral cavity. Other muscles in the suprahyoid group, while important for swallowing and tongue movement, do not collectively form this primary muscular base. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q25: The Circle of Willis is an important anastomosis of arteries located where?**

- A) In the heart 
- B) At the base of the brain ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) In the liver 
- D) Around the kidneys 

*Explanation:* Great effort on this question! You're absolutely right that the Circle of Willis is found **at the base of the brain**. This critical arterial anastomosis forms a protective ring, ensuring continuous blood supply to the brain even if there's a blockage in one of the major arteries supplying it. Therefore, any other location, such as the heart or kidneys, would be incorrect as its function is exclusively to cerebral circulation.

---

**Q26: The nasopalatine nerve passes through which foramen?**

- A) Greater palatine foramen 
- B) Lesser palatine foramen 
- C) Incisive foramen ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Sphenopalatine foramen 

*Explanation:* You're doing great! The **incisive foramen** is indeed the correct answer because the nasopalatine nerve uniquely travels through this canal to emerge onto the anterior hard palate, supplying sensation to the palatal mucosa from canine to canine. Other foramina in the region, such as the greater and lesser palatine foramina, transmit different nerves responsible for the posterior hard and soft palate, respectively, not the nasopalatine. Keep up the fantastic work reviewing these anatomical pathways!

---

**Q27: Which of these is NOT a muscle of mastication?**

- A) Masseter 
- B) Temporalis 
- C) Buccinator ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Medial pterygoid 

*Explanation:* Great job tackling that question! The Buccinator is indeed the correct answer because it's primarily a muscle of facial expression, innervated by the facial nerve (VII), helping with functions like smiling and keeping food on the occlusal surfaces. The true muscles of mastication (Masseter, Temporalis, Medial Pterygoid, and Lateral Pterygoid) are distinct, primarily responsible for jaw movements, and are innervated by the trigeminal nerve (V3). Keep up the excellent work distinguishing these important muscle groups!

---

**Q28: The periodontal ligament is composed primarily of which type of collagen?**

- A) Type I ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Type II 
- C) Type III 
- D) Type IV 

*Explanation:* No worries, this is a common point of confusion!

You're doing great! Type I collagen is the correct answer because it is the most abundant and provides the high tensile strength necessary for the periodontal ligament to firmly anchor the tooth and withstand strong occlusal forces. While other collagen types are present in the PDL, they play secondary roles; Type I is the dominant structural component responsible for its robust mechanical function. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q29: Which cells are responsible for producing enamel?**

- A) Odontoblasts 
- B) Ameloblasts ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Cementoblasts 
- D) Fibroblasts 

*Explanation:* No worries, this is a common point of confusion! Ameloblasts are the *only* cells responsible for synthesizing and secreting enamel during tooth development. Remember, each "-blast" cell type is specialized: odontoblasts form dentin, cementoblasts form cementum, and fibroblasts produce connective tissues like collagen, not hard dental tissues. Keep up the great work!

---

**Q30: The chorda tympani nerve is a branch of which cranial nerve?**

- A) CN V 
- B) CN VII ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) CN IX 
- D) CN X 

*Explanation:* Great effort! The chorda tympani nerve is a special visceral efferent and afferent branch of the **facial nerve (CN VII)**, which arises within the temporal bone and carries taste sensation from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue, as well as preganglionic parasympathetic fibers to the submandibular and sublingual glands. Other cranial nerves like CN V, IX, or X have distinct branches and innervation territories and do not give rise to the chorda tympani. Keep up the excellent work; you're doing great!

---

**Q31: Which sinus is most likely to be perforated during an extraction of a maxillary molar?**

- A) Frontal sinus 
- B) Ethmoid sinus 
- C) Sphenoid sinus 
- D) Maxillary sinus ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* That's a very common point of confusion, but you're doing great! The maxillary sinus is indeed the correct answer because its floor often extends to intimately surround or lie immediately superior to the roots of the maxillary molars, making it highly susceptible to perforation during extraction. Other sinuses like the frontal, sphenoid, or ethmoid are located more superiorly or posteriorly and are anatomically distant from the maxillary posterior teeth, thus not at risk during these extractions. Keep up the excellent work!

---

**Q32: The Wharton's duct drains which salivary gland?**

- A) Parotid gland 
- B) Sublingual gland 
- C) Submandibular gland ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Labial glands 

*Explanation:* You're doing great as you review these anatomical details! Wharton's duct, also known as the submandibular duct, specifically drains saliva from the submandibular gland onto the floor of the mouth. Remember that the parotid gland uses Stensen's duct, and the sublingual gland has multiple smaller ducts (Rivinus') and sometimes a larger one (Bartholin's), making Wharton's duct unique to the submandibular gland.

---

**Q33: The hamular process is part of which bone?**

- A) Maxilla 
- B) Mandible 
- C) Sphenoid bone ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Palatine bone 

*Explanation:* You're on the right track, keep up the great work! The hamular process, also known as the pterygoid hamulus, is a distinct hook-like projection that extends from the inferior end of the medial pterygoid plate. This plate is an integral part of the pterygoid process, which itself is a major inferior extension of the sphenoid bone.

---

**Q34: The inferior alveolar nerve is a branch of which nerve?**

- A) Maxillary nerve 
- B) Mandibular nerve ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Ophthalmic nerve 
- D) Facial nerve 

*Explanation:* You're on the right track! The inferior alveolar nerve is a direct and major sensory branch of the mandibular nerve (V3). While the mandibular nerve is one of three divisions of the trigeminal nerve, the IAN specifically originates from V3, distinguishing it from branches of the ophthalmic (V1) or maxillary (V2) divisions.

---

**Q35: Which structure is located within the cavernous sinus?**

- A) Optic nerve 
- B) Abducens nerve ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Facial nerve 
- D) Trigeminal ganglion 

*Explanation:* Great effort on that question! The abducens nerve (CN VI) is unique in that it's the *only* cranial nerve that truly runs freely through the cavernous sinus, bathed directly in its venous blood. Other cranial nerves like the oculomotor (CN III), trochlear (CN IV), ophthalmic (CN V1), and maxillary (CN V2) nerves are typically embedded within the *lateral wall* of the sinus, not directly within its lumen, making the abducens nerve the correct answer. Keep up the excellent work!

---

## Topic: PROSTHODONTICS

**Q595: What is the primary purpose of a diagnostic cast?**

- A) To fabricate the final prosthesis 
- B) For patient education and treatment planning ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) To check the occlusion directly in the mouth 
- D) To serve as a temporary restoration 

---

**Q596: The component of a removable partial denture that provides resistance to displacement away from the tissues is the:**

- A) Rest 
- B) Major connector 
- C) Direct retainer (clasp) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Denture base 

---

**Q597: Which term describes a complete denture that is fabricated for insertion immediately after the removal of natural teeth?**

- A) Interim denture 
- B) Immediate denture ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Conventional denture 
- D) Overdenture 

---

**Q598: The imaginary line connecting the canines, used to help determine the position of the anterior teeth, is called the:**

- A) Canine line ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Smile line 
- C) Interpupillary line 
- D) Lip line 

---

**Q599: What is the primary function of a post and core?**

- A) To strengthen the tooth root 
- B) To retain a core for a final crown ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) To seal the root canal 
- D) To improve the color of the tooth 

---

**Q600: The part of a fixed partial denture that replaces the missing tooth is called a:**

- A) Retainer 
- B) Pontic ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Connector 
- D) Abutment 

---

**Q601: In complete dentures, 'centric relation' refers to:**

- A) The patient's habitual bite 
- B) The most retruded position of the mandible ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) A tooth-guided position 
- D) The position of maximum intercuspation 

---

**Q602: The part of a removable partial denture that connects the components on one side of the arch to the other is the:**

- A) Denture base 
- B) Rest 
- C) Direct retainer 
- D) Major connector ✅ **(CORRECT)**

---

**Q603: An abutment is a tooth or implant that:**

- A) Is missing 
- B) Supports a prosthesis ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Has a large filling 
- D) Is decayed 

---

**Q604: The border of a complete denture is determined by:**

- A) The dentist's preference 
- B) The size of the teeth 
- C) The functional movements of the surrounding muscles ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) The height of the alveolar ridge 

---

**Q605: What is Ante's Law in fixed prosthodontics?**

- A) A law governing the path of insertion 
- B) A guideline stating the root surface area of abutment teeth should equal or exceed that of the teeth being replaced ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) A rule for selecting the shade of a crown 
- D) A principle of occlusal adjustment 

---

**Q606: A Kennedy Class I partial denture classification indicates:**

- A) Unilateral edentulous area posterior to the remaining teeth 
- B) Bilateral edentulous areas posterior to the remaining teeth ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) A unilateral edentulous area with natural teeth both anterior and posterior to it 
- D) A single, bilateral edentulous area crossing the midline 

---

**Q607: The 'ferrule effect' refers to:**

- A) The type of cement used 
- B) A band of coronal tooth structure that a crown encircles, providing fracture resistance ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) The polished surface of a denture 
- D) The retention of a clasp 

---

**Q608: What is the primary purpose of an occlusal rest on a removable partial denture?**

- A) To provide retention 
- B) To prevent gingival displacement of the prosthesis ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) To provide bracing 
- D) To improve esthetics 

---

**Q609: In complete denture fabrication, the vertical dimension of occlusion (VDO) is:**

- A) The vertical position of the mandible when the teeth are in maximum intercuspation ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) The vertical position of the mandible at rest 
- C) Always 3mm less than the vertical dimension at rest 
- D) Determined by the patient's smile 

---

**Q610: A hygienic or sanitary pontic design is characterized by:**

- A) Resting heavily on the ridge 
- B) Being easy to clean, with no contact with the ridge ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Having a pointed shape 
- D) Being made of porcelain 

---

**Q611: Which impression material is best suited for making a final impression for a complete denture?**

- A) Alginate 
- B) Impression compound

- D) Wax 

---

**Q612: What is the Bennett angle?**

- A) The angle of the articular eminence

- D) The angle of the cusps on the posterior teeth 

---

**Q613: A non-adjustable articulator is also known as a:**

- A) Semi-adjustable articulator

**Q782: What is the main function of red blood cells?**

- A) To fight infection 
- B) To assist in clotting 
- C) To transport oxygen ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) To produce energy 

---

**Q783: What is the primary risk of treating a patient with a high INR?**

- A) Thrombosis (clotting) 
- B) Hemorrhage (bleeding) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Infection 
- D) Allergic reaction 

---

**Q784: Guillain-Barré syndrome is a disorder characterized by:**

- A) Chronic descending paralysis 
- B) Rapidly ascending paralysis caused by autoimmune attack on peripheral nerves ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Progressive dementia 
- D) A seizure disorder 

---

**Q785: Which of these is the most common cause of end-stage renal disease in the United States?**

- A) Hypertension 
- B) Polycystic kidney disease 
- C) Glomerulonephritis 
- D) Diabetes ✅ **(CORRECT)**

**Q786: A patient who faints in the dental chair should be placed in what position?**

- A) Upright 
- B) Trendelenburg (head down, feet up) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Standing 
- D) On their side 

---

**Q787: Which blood-borne virus has a vaccine available for prevention?**

- A) Hepatitis C 
- B) HIV 
- C) Hepatitis B ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) All of the above 

---

**Q788: Porphyria is a group of genetic disorders that result from a buildup of natural chemicals that produce porphyrin in your body. What is porphyrin essential for?**

- A) Hemoglobin function ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Nerve conduction 
- C) Muscle contraction 
- D) Hormone synthesis 

---

**Q789: A patient with GERD should be advised to avoid which position in the dental chair for long periods?**

- A) Upright 
- B) Supine ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) On their left side 
- D) Standing 

---

**Q790: What is the term for a cancerous tumor that has not invaded nearby tissue?**

- A) Metastatic 
- B) Benign 
- C) In situ ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Invasive 

---

**Q791: The most common cause of community-acquired pneumonia is:**

- A) Haemophilus influenzae 
- B) Mycoplasma pneumoniae 
- C) Streptococcus pneumoniae ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Legionella pneumophila 

---

**Q792: Which of the following is a key pathophysiological feature of emphysema?**

- A) Bronchoconstriction 
- B) Destruction of alveolar walls ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Inflammation of the bronchioles 
- D) Excessive mucus production 

---

**Q793: What is the primary role of an HDL cholesterol particle?**

- A) Transport dietary fat 
- B) Transport cholesterol to peripheral tissues 
- C) Transport cholesterol from peripheral tissues back to the liver ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Form plaques in arteries 

---

**Q794: An ischemic stroke is caused by:**

- A) A rupture of a blood vessel in the brain 
- B) A blockage of a blood vessel in the brain ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) A severe migraine 
- D) A brain tumor

## Topic: ETHICS

**Q795: The principle of "doing no harm" is known as:**

- A) Beneficence 
- B) Autonomy 
- C) Justice 
- D) Non-maleficence ✅ **(CORRECT)**

---

**Q796: Which ethical principle is primarily involved in obtaining informed consent?**

- A) Justice 
- B) Autonomy ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Beneficence 
- D) Veracity 

---

**Q797: The principle of "truthfulness" and being honest with patients is:**

- A) Fidelity 
- B) Veracity ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Autonomy 
- D) Justice 

---

**Q798: Treating all patients fairly and equitably falls under the principle of:**

- A) Beneficence 
- B) Justice ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Non-maleficence 
- D) Autonomy 

---

**Q799: The principle of acting for the "benefit of the patient" is known as:**

- A) Justice 
- B) Fidelity 
- C) Autonomy 
- D) Beneficence ✅ **(CORRECT)**

---

**Q800: A dentist who refers a patient to a specialist and receives a portion of the specialist's fee is engaging in:**

- A) Fee splitting ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) A reasonable referral practice 
- C) Patient advocacy 
- D) Risk management 

---

**Q801: If a dentist notices a colleague is practicing while impaired, what is their primary ethical obligation?**

- A) Ignore the situation to avoid conflict 
- B) Protect the colleague from losing their license 
- C) Report the colleague to the appropriate authorities (e.g., state dental board) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Take over the colleague's patients 

---

**Q802: Performing a procedure that is unnecessary for the patient's health, but is profitable for the dentist, is a violation of which principles?**

- A) Beneficence and Veracity 
- B) Autonomy and Justice 
- C) Non-maleficence and Justice 
- D) Beneficence and Non-maleficence ✅ **(CORRECT)**

---

**Q803: What is the term for a situation where a dentist has a duty to act but fails to do so, resulting in harm to the patient?**

- A) Assault 
- B) Battery 
- C) Negligence ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Abandonment 

---

**Q804: A dentist terminates care of a patient without giving the patient reasonable notice or the opportunity to find a new provider. This is known as:**

- A) Referral 
- B) Battery 
- C) Abandonment ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Standard of care 

---

**Q805: Patient confidentiality is a key aspect of which ethical duty?**

- A) Veracity 
- B) Autonomy ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Beneficence 
- D) Fidelity 

---

**Q806: A dentist's primary ethical duty is to the:**

- A) Insurance company 
- B) Patient ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Dental staff 
- D) Profession as a whole 

---

**Q807: Informed consent must include all of the following EXCEPT:**

- A) The nature of the proposed treatment 
- B) The risks and benefits of the treatment 
- C) Alternative treatment options 
- D) A guarantee of a successful outcome ✅ **(CORRECT)**

---

**Q808: Advertising that is misleading or makes false claims violates the principle of:**

- A) Justice 
- B) Veracity ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Autonomy 
- D) Beneficence 

---

**Q809: Paternalism in a dental context is when the dentist:**

- A) Acts like a father figure to the staff 
- B) Makes a decision for the patient based on what the dentist believes is in their best interest, overriding the patient's autonomy ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Treats all patients as if they were family 
- D) Refuses to treat patients who are parents 

---

**Q810: What is "upcoding"?**

- A) Charging a higher fee for a more complex procedure than the one actually performed ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Using a new dental code 
- C) Charging the same fee for all patients 
- D) Giving a patient a discount 

---

**Q811: Keeping one's skills and knowledge current through continuing education is an example of which ethical principle?**

- A) Competence ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Justice 
- C) Veracity 
- D) Autonomy 

---

**Q812: What is the ethical consideration when a dentist owns a financial interest in a dental lab they refer patients to?**

- A) Conflict of interest ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Standard of care 
- C) Fee splitting 
- D) Abandonment 

---

**Q813: A patient refuses a recommended treatment. The dentist should:**

- A) Perform the treatment anyway 
- B) Respect the patient's decision after ensuring they are fully informed ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Dismiss the patient from the practice immediately 
- D) Only offer that one treatment option 

---

**Q814: Which of the following is an example of battery in a dental office?**

- A) Charging a patient for a missed appointment 
- B) Speaking rudely to a patient 
- C) Performing a procedure on a patient without their consent ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Having a messy waiting room 

---

**Q815: A dentist suggests a treatment that is not the best option for the patient, but is covered by their insurance. This creates a conflict between:**

- A) Beneficence and patient autonomy 
- B) The dentist's financial interest and the patient's best interest ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Justice and veracity 
- D) Non-maleficence and competence 

---

**Q816: The "standard of care" is defined as what a:**

- A) Reasonable and prudent dentist would do in a similar situation ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Specialist would do in any situation 
- C) Textbook says is the ideal treatment 
- D) Lawyer defines as the minimum requirement 

---

**Q817: Respecting a patient's right to self-determination is the principle of:**

- A) Autonomy ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Justice 
- C) Veracity 
- D) Fidelity 

---

**Q818: If a dentist makes a mistake during treatment, what is their ethical obligation?**

- A) Ignore it and hope the patient doesn't notice 
- B) Blame the assistant 
- C) Inform the patient of the error and the consequences ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Fix it without telling the patient 

---

**Q819: A "therapeutic privilege" is the rare situation where a provider may be justified in:**

- A) Withholding information from a patient if they believe the information would cause serious harm ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Performing treatment without consent 
- C) Charging a higher fee for a difficult patient 
- D) Refusing to treat a patient 

---

**Q820: Which of the following is a primary goal of a dental practice act?**

- A) To set fees for dental services 
- B) To protect the public from incompetent practitioners ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) To advertise for dentists 
- D) To provide jobs for dental staff 

---

**Q821: HIPAA is a law in the United States that primarily deals with:**

- A) Billing practices 
- B) Infection control 
- C) Patient privacy and confidentiality ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Advertising 

---

**Q822: If a minor patient needs urgent treatment and the parents cannot be reached, the dentist may be able to treat the patient under the concept of:**

- A) Expressed consent 
- B) Informed refusal 
- C) Implied consent (emergency doctrine) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Therapeutic privilege 

---

**Q823: What is the difference between "libel" and "slander"?**

- A) Libel is spoken, slander is written 
- B) Libel is written, slander is spoken ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) They are the same thing 
- D) Libel involves patients, slander involves colleagues 

---

**Q824: A dentist who criticizes the work of another dentist to a patient in a false or misleading way is engaging in:**

- C) Patient education

---

**Q825: Which principle requires that the benefits of a treatment outweigh its risks?**

- A) Justice 
- B) Veracity 
- C) Autonomy 
- D) Beneficence/Non-maleficence ✅ **(CORRECT)**

---

**Q826: The legal doctrine "res ipsa loquitur" means:**

- A) The thing speaks for itself ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Let the master answer 
- C) Do no harm 
- D) The patient is always right 

---

**Q827: A dentist is asked by a patient to pre-date an insurance claim form. The dentist should:**

---

**Q828: Ethical codes for dentists are primarily created and enforced by:**

- A) The government 
- B) Professional organizations (e.g., ADA, CDA) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Insurance companies 
- D) Lawyers

**Q829: If a patient requests a copy of their dental records, the dentist:**

- A) Can refuse the request

---

- D) The patient 

---

**Q831: Treating a patient with respect and dignity falls under which broad ethical category?**

- A) Professionalism ✅ **(CORRECT)**

- C) Advertising 
- D) Record keeping 

---

**Q832: The use of "before and after" photos in advertising is:**

**Q833: What is a "statute of limitations" in a dental malpractice case?**

**Q834: A dentist offering a discount to a patient for paying in cash and then not reporting that income is an example of:**

- D) To create laws

---

**Q837: A dentist has a new associate. The senior dentist is ultimately responsible for all care provided in the practice under the doctrine of:**

- D) Statute of limitations 

---

**Q838: Which of the following is NOT an element a patient must prove in a malpractice case?**

**Q839: Which principle relates to the dentist's duty to be a steward of the community?**

- D) Veracity

- D) Justice

---

**Q851: If a child is a victim of suspected abuse or neglect, the dentist has a legal and ethical obligation to:**

**Q853: The concept that a community has a right to the same quality of care as individuals is an aspect of:**

**Q854: Providing a patient with sufficient information to make a decision about their treatment upholds the principle of:**

**Q855: A dentist develops a new technique and publishes the results, but fails to mention a financial interest in the company that produces the materials used. This is a failure to disclose a:**

**Q856: A "Good Samaritan" law generally protects a healthcare provider who:**

**Q871: What is the main ethical objection to a "product-based" sales approach in dentistry?**

**Q891: Telling a patient a filling needs to be replaced when it is clinically sound is a violation of:**

**Q893: What is the term for the ethical and legal responsibility for one's actions?**

**Q894: A "dual relationship" is an ethical concern because it can:**

- A) Improve patient care 
- B) Blur professional boundaries and impair judgment ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Help the dentist make more money 
- D) Make the patient more comfortable 

---


## Topic: ANATOMY

**Q895: A patient presents with paralysis of the muscles of facial expression on the right side, including an inability to close the right eye and an absent corneal reflex on the right. Which cranial nerve is most likely affected?**

- A) Trigeminal nerve (CN V) 
- B) Facial nerve (CN VII) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Oculomotor nerve (CN III) 
- D) Abducens nerve (CN VI) 

*Explanation:* The facial nerve (CN VII) primarily innervates the muscles of facial expression, and its paralysis leads to an inability to close the eye. While the afferent limb of the corneal reflex is the trigeminal nerve (CN V), the efferent limb responsible for eyelid closure is the facial nerve (CN VII), making it the affected nerve in this scenario.

---

**Q896: Which of the following muscles is primarily responsible for the *retraction* of the mandible?**

- A) Lateral pterygoid muscle 
- B) Medial pterygoid muscle 
- C) Temporalis muscle ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Masseter muscle 

*Explanation:* The posterior fibers of the temporalis muscle are primarily responsible for retracting the mandible. The masseter and medial pterygoid muscles are strong elevators, while the lateral pterygoid muscle is mainly involved in protrusion and depression.

---

**Q897: Which artery is the principal blood supply to the anterior two-thirds of the tongue?**

- A) Facial artery 
- B) Maxillary artery 
- C) Lingual artery ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Superior thyroid artery 

*Explanation:* The lingual artery, a direct branch of the external carotid artery, is the primary blood supply to the entire tongue, including its anterior two-thirds via its dorsal lingual and deep lingual branches. The facial and maxillary arteries supply other regions of the face and deep structures, respectively.

---

**Q898: The parotid duct (Stensen's duct) typically opens into the oral cavity adjacent to which tooth?**

- A) Maxillary central incisor 
- B) Mandibular first premolar 
- C) Maxillary second molar ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Mandibular canine 

*Explanation:* Stensen's duct, which drains saliva from the parotid gland, consistently opens into the oral vestibule on a small papilla located opposite the crown of the maxillary second molar. This anatomical landmark is crucial for clinical examinations and procedures.

---

**Q899: Which ligament of the temporomandibular joint (TMJ) prevents excessive posterior movement of the condyle and reinforces the lateral aspect of the joint capsule?**

- A) Sphenomandibular ligament 
- B) Stylomandibular ligament 
- C) Temporomandibular ligament (lateral ligament) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Pterygomandibular raphe 

*Explanation:* The temporomandibular ligament (or lateral ligament) is a strong thickening of the fibrous capsule of the TMJ. It plays a critical role in limiting posterior and inferior movements of the condyle, thereby protecting the joint from dislocation and trauma.

---

**Q900: Taste buds are primarily found within which type of lingual papillae on the dorsal surface of the tongue?**

- A) Filiform papillae 
- B) Fungiform papillae 
- C) Foliate papillae 
- D) Circumvallate papillae ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* While fungiform and foliate papillae also contain taste buds, the circumvallate papillae, arranged in a V-shape at the posterior of the tongue, house a significant number of taste buds along their lateral walls. Filiform papillae are the most numerous but lack taste buds, primarily serving a mechanical function.

---

**Q901: Which paranasal sinus is most closely related to the apices of the maxillary posterior teeth, making it clinically relevant during dental procedures like extractions or implant placement?**

- A) Frontal sinus 
- B) Sphenoid sinus 
- C) Ethmoid sinus 
- D) Maxillary sinus ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* The maxillary sinus is located directly superior to the posterior maxillary teeth, and the roots of molars and premolars often project into its floor. This close anatomical relationship necessitates careful consideration during dental procedures to prevent complications such as oroantral communication.

---

**Q902: Which nerve provides general sensation to the buccal gingiva of the mandibular molars?**

- A) Lingual nerve 
- B) Mental nerve 
- C) Long buccal nerve (Buccal nerve, branch of V3) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Inferior alveolar nerve 

*Explanation:* The long buccal nerve, a branch of the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve (V3), provides general sensation to the buccal gingiva and mucosa in the mandibular molar region. The inferior alveolar nerve innervates the mandibular teeth, while the lingual nerve supplies the lingual gingiva.

---

**Q903: Which of the following structures passes through the incisive foramen in the maxilla?**

- A) Nasopalatine nerve and sphenopalatine artery ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Greater palatine nerve and artery 
- C) Lesser palatine nerve and artery 
- D) Posterior superior alveolar nerve and artery 

*Explanation:* The incisive foramen, located in the anterior hard palate, serves as a passageway for the nasopalatine nerve and the terminal branches of the greater palatine artery (often referred to as the sphenopalatine artery). These structures provide sensory innervation and blood supply to the anterior palate.

---

**Q904: The primary lymphatic drainage for the lower lip and chin typically involves which group of lymph nodes?**

- A) Submandibular lymph nodes 
- B) Deep cervical lymph nodes 
- C) Submental lymph nodes ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Jugulodigastric lymph nodes 

*Explanation:* The submental lymph nodes are the initial drainage site for lymphatic fluid from the central part of the lower lip, the chin, and the anterior floor of the mouth. Lymph then flows from these nodes to the submandibular and subsequently the deep cervical lymph nodes.

---

**Q905: Which muscle forms the primary bulk of the soft palate and is responsible for tensing the soft palate and opening the Eustachian tube during swallowing and yawning?**

- A) Palatoglossus muscle 
- B) Palatopharyngeus muscle 
- C) Levator veli palatini muscle 
- D) Tensor veli palatini muscle ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* The tensor veli palatini muscle is crucial for tensing the soft palate and, uniquely among the palatal muscles, for opening the auditory (Eustachian) tube to equalize middle ear pressure. The levator veli palatini primarily elevates the soft palate.

---

**Q906: An infection originating from a mandibular molar with roots extending below the mylohyoid muscle is most likely to spread initially into which fascial space?**

- A) Sublingual space 
- B) Submandibular space ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Pterygomandibular space 
- D) Parapharyngeal space 

*Explanation:* The mylohyoid muscle acts as a barrier, separating the sublingual space (above the muscle) from the submandibular space (below the muscle). Therefore, if a mandibular molar infection perforates the cortical bone apical to the mylohyoid line, it will typically drain into and spread through the submandibular space.

---


## Topic: PHARMACOLOGY

**Q907: A 68-year-old male patient with a history of atrial fibrillation is currently taking warfarin. He presents for an extraction of tooth #30 and requires post-operative pain management. Which of the following analgesics is *most appropriate* to prescribe for this patient?**

- A) Acetaminophen 500 mg every 4-6 hours as needed. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Ibuprofen 400 mg every 6 hours as needed. 
- C) Aspirin 325 mg every 4 hours as needed. 
- D) Meloxicam 7.5 mg daily. 

*Explanation:* Acetaminophen is the analgesic of choice for patients on warfarin as it has minimal effect on platelet function and does not significantly increase the risk of bleeding. NSAIDs like ibuprofen, aspirin, and meloxicam (even selective COX-2 inhibitors) can inhibit platelet aggregation and increase the risk of hemorrhage when combined with anticoagulants.

---

**Q908: A 45-year-old female patient with a prosthetic heart valve requires antibiotic prophylaxis for infective endocarditis prior to a dental extraction. She reports a history of a 'mild rash' when taking amoxicillin in the past. Which of the following is the *most appropriate* antibiotic to prescribe for this patient?**

- A) Azithromycin 500 mg orally, 1 hour prior to the procedure. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Amoxicillin 2g orally, 1 hour prior to the procedure. 
- C) Cephalexin 2g orally, 1 hour prior to the procedure. 
- D) Metronidazole 500 mg orally, 1 hour prior to the procedure. 

*Explanation:* For patients with a penicillin allergy experiencing a non-anaphylactic reaction (like a mild rash), macrolides such as azithromycin are a recommended alternative for infective endocarditis prophylaxis. Amoxicillin is contraindicated due to the allergy. Cephalexin has a low risk of cross-reactivity with penicillin but is generally avoided if other suitable alternatives are available to completely eliminate risk, and metronidazole is not indicated for IE prophylaxis.

---

**Q909: A 72-year-old male patient presents for multiple restorations. His medical history includes uncontrolled hypertension (BP 160/100 mmHg) and a myocardial infarction 6 months ago. Which local anesthetic regimen is *most appropriate* for this patient?**

- A) Lidocaine 2% with 1:50,000 epinephrine. 
- B) Mepivacaine 3% without vasoconstrictor. 
- C) Articaine 4% with 1:100,000 epinephrine, limited to 2 carpules. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Bupivacaine 0.5% with 1:200,000 epinephrine. 

*Explanation:* For patients with significant cardiovascular compromise, it's crucial to minimize vasoconstrictor dose. Articaine 4% with 1:100,000 epinephrine, limited to 2 carpules, provides effective anesthesia while keeping the epinephrine dose (0.036 mg) below the cardiac limit (0.04 mg). Lidocaine with 1:50,000 epinephrine contains too much epinephrine. Mepivacaine without a vasoconstrictor might lead to inadequate anesthesia and increased endogenous catecholamine release from pain, which can be more detrimental. Bupivacaine has higher cardiac toxicity and a longer duration not typically needed for restorations.

---


## Topic: BIOCHEMISTRY

**Q910: A patient presents with a rare genetic disorder affecting erythrocyte metabolism, characterized by chronic hemolytic anemia. Analysis reveals a defect in an enzyme crucial for the irreversible commitment step of glycolysis. Which enzyme is most likely deficient?**

- A) Phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Hexokinase 
- C) Pyruvate kinase 
- D) Aldolase 

*Explanation:* PFK-1 catalyzes the phosphorylation of fructose-6-phosphate to fructose-1,6-bisphosphate, which is the committed and rate-limiting step of glycolysis. A deficiency in PFK-1 would severely impair glycolysis, leading to insufficient ATP production for erythrocytes, resulting in hemolytic anemia. While other enzyme deficiencies can also cause hemolytic anemia, PFK-1 is specifically associated with the 'irreversible commitment step'.

---

**Q911: A 35-year-old patient presents with bleeding gums, impaired wound healing, and petechiae. Dietary history reveals very poor intake of fruits and vegetables. Which of the following post-translational modifications of collagen is most likely impaired in this patient?**

- A) Glycosylation of hydroxylysine residues 
- B) Hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Disulfide bond formation between procollagen chains 
- D) Cleavage of propeptides by procollagen peptidases 

*Explanation:* The patient's symptoms are classic for scurvy, caused by Vitamin C deficiency. Vitamin C (ascorbate) is a co-factor for prolyl hydroxylase and lysyl hydroxylase, enzymes critical for the hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues in collagen. This hydroxylation is essential for forming stable cross-links and the mature triple helix structure of collagen; without it, collagen is unstable, leading to connective tissue defects.

---

**Q912: A neonate presents with lethargy, poor feeding, and hyperammonemia. Laboratory tests reveal elevated citrulline levels and normal argininosuccinate levels in plasma. Which enzyme deficiency in the urea cycle is most consistent with these findings?**

- A) Carbamoyl phosphate synthetase I (CPS-I) 
- B) Ornithine transcarbamylase (OTC) 
- C) Argininosuccinate synthetase (ASS) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Argininosuccinate lyase (ASL) 

*Explanation:* Elevated citrulline and normal argininosuccinate levels indicate a block *after* citrulline is formed but *before* argininosuccinate is processed. Argininosuccinate synthetase (ASS) converts citrulline and aspartate to argininosuccinate. A deficiency in ASS would cause citrulline to accumulate. CPS-I and OTC deficiencies would lead to low or absent citrulline, while ASL deficiency would lead to elevated argininosuccinate.

---

**Q913: A patient with glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency experiences hemolytic anemia after consuming fava beans. The primary reason for the increased susceptibility to oxidative stress in their red blood cells is the impaired production of which crucial molecule?**

- A) ATP 
- B) NADH 
- C) NADPH ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) FADH2 

*Explanation:* G6PD is the rate-limiting enzyme of the pentose phosphate pathway, which generates NADPH. NADPH is essential for reducing oxidized glutathione, a key antioxidant in red blood cells that protects against reactive oxygen species. Without sufficient NADPH, red blood cells are vulnerable to oxidative damage, leading to hemolysis.

---

**Q914: A 1-year-old child presents with hepatomegaly, severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, and hyperlipidemia. Muscle weakness is NOT a prominent feature. Which enzyme deficiency is most likely responsible for this condition?**

- A) Glycogen phosphorylase 
- B) Glucose-6-phosphatase ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase) 
- D) Lysosomal alpha-1,4-glucosidase (acid maltase) 

*Explanation:* This clinical picture describes Glycogen Storage Disease Type I (Von Gierke disease), caused by a deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase. This enzyme is crucial for releasing free glucose from the liver during glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, leading to severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, and hepatomegaly due to glycogen accumulation. Muscle weakness is absent because the enzyme is primarily expressed in the liver and kidney.

---

**Q915: During DNA replication, which enzyme is responsible for synthesizing short RNA primers that provide a free 3'-OH group for DNA polymerase to extend?**

- A) DNA ligase 
- B) DNA polymerase I 
- C) Primase ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Helicase 

*Explanation:* Primase is a specialized RNA polymerase that synthesizes short RNA primers on the DNA template. These primers are necessary because DNA polymerase can only add nucleotides to an existing 3'-OH group. DNA ligase seals nicks, DNA polymerase I removes RNA primers and fills gaps, and helicase unwinds the DNA double helix.

---

**Q916: A novel drug is found to decrease the apparent Vmax of an enzyme but does not alter its Km. What type of enzyme inhibition does this drug most likely exhibit?**

- A) Competitive inhibition 
- B) Noncompetitive inhibition ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Uncompetitive inhibition 
- D) Irreversible inhibition 

*Explanation:* Noncompetitive inhibition is characterized by the inhibitor binding to an allosteric site on the enzyme, altering its conformation and reducing its catalytic efficiency. This leads to a decrease in the apparent Vmax, as the enzyme's maximal velocity is reduced, but the Km (substrate affinity) remains unchanged because the inhibitor does not directly compete with the substrate for the active site.

---

**Q917: Which of the following is the direct product of one cycle of beta-oxidation of a saturated fatty acyl-CoA molecule?**

- A) Acetyl-CoA, NADH, and FADH2 ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Malonyl-CoA, NADH, and FADH2 
- C) Propionyl-CoA, NADH, and FADH2 
- D) Succinyl-CoA, NADH, and FADH2 

*Explanation:* Each round of beta-oxidation shortens the fatty acyl-CoA chain by two carbons, releasing one molecule of Acetyl-CoA. Additionally, one molecule of FADH2 and one molecule of NADH are produced, carrying electrons to the electron transport chain for ATP synthesis. Malonyl-CoA is involved in fatty acid synthesis, while Propionyl-CoA and Succinyl-CoA are intermediates in the oxidation of odd-chain fatty acids.

---

**Q918: The rate-limiting step in cholesterol biosynthesis is catalyzed by which enzyme, and what is its primary regulatory mechanism?**

- A) Squalene synthase; inhibited by statin drugs 
- B) HMG-CoA reductase; inhibited by statin drugs ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Acyl-CoA:cholesterol acyltransferase (ACAT); activated by high cholesterol 
- D) Cholesterol 7-alpha-hydroxylase; feedback inhibited by bile acids 

*Explanation:* HMG-CoA reductase catalyzes the conversion of HMG-CoA to mevalonate, which is the committed and rate-limiting step in cholesterol biosynthesis. This enzyme is the primary target for statin drugs, which competitively inhibit it to lower cholesterol levels. Squalene synthase is later in the pathway, ACAT esterifies cholesterol for storage, and cholesterol 7-alpha-hydroxylase is involved in bile acid synthesis, not cholesterol synthesis regulation.

---

**Q919: Which of the following statements about glycosaminoglycans (GAGs) is FALSE?**

- A) They are long, unbranched polysaccharides containing repeating disaccharide units. 
- B) They are highly negatively charged due to sulfate and carboxyl groups. 
- C) They are major components of the extracellular matrix and contribute to its turgor. 
- D) Hyaluronic acid is the only GAG that is covalently linked to a protein core to form a proteoglycan. ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* Hyaluronic acid is unique among GAGs because it is *not* covalently linked to a protein core to form a proteoglycan. Instead, it forms very large aggregates by associating with other proteoglycans. All other GAGs (chondroitin sulfate, dermatan sulfate, heparan sulfate, keratan sulfate) are covalently attached to protein cores to form proteoglycans.

---

**Q920: Following a carbohydrate-rich meal, which of the following metabolic events is primarily stimulated by insulin?**

- A) Glycogenolysis in the liver 
- B) Gluconeogenesis in the liver 
- C) Fatty acid oxidation in adipose tissue 
- D) Glucose uptake and utilization by muscle and adipose tissue ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* Insulin is an anabolic hormone released in response to high blood glucose. Its primary role is to promote glucose uptake by insulin-sensitive tissues like muscle and adipose tissue (via GLUT4 translocation) and to stimulate glucose utilization (glycolysis) and storage (glycogenesis, lipogenesis). Glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis are inhibited by insulin and stimulated by glucagon.

---

**Q921: A chronic alcoholic patient presents with Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome, characterized by ataxia, ophthalmoplegia, and memory loss. This condition is primarily due to a deficiency of which vitamin, acting as a coenzyme for key metabolic enzymes?**

- A) Vitamin B1 (Thiamine) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- B) Vitamin B2 (Riboflavin) 
- C) Vitamin B3 (Niacin) 
- D) Vitamin B6 (Pyridoxine) 

*Explanation:* Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome is a severe neurological disorder caused by thiamine (Vitamin B1) deficiency, common in chronic alcoholism due to poor nutrition and impaired absorption. Thiamine pyrophosphate is a crucial coenzyme for pyruvate dehydrogenase, alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, and transketolase, enzymes vital for carbohydrate metabolism and neurotransmitter synthesis in the brain.

---

**Q922: Cyanide poisoning is lethal because it inhibits which component of the electron transport chain, leading to a rapid cessation of ATP synthesis?**

- A) Complex I (NADH dehydrogenase) 
- B) Complex II (Succinate dehydrogenase) 
- C) Complex III (Cytochrome bc1 complex) 
- D) Complex IV (Cytochrome c oxidase) ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* Cyanide is a potent inhibitor of Complex IV (cytochrome c oxidase) in the electron transport chain. By binding to the ferric iron in cytochrome a3, it prevents the transfer of electrons to oxygen, the final electron acceptor. This halts the entire electron transport chain, dissipates the proton gradient, and thus stops oxidative phosphorylation and ATP production, leading to cellular anoxia.

---

**Q923: A newborn screening test reveals elevated phenylalanine levels. This indicates a diagnosis of Phenylketonuria (PKU), which is caused by a deficiency in which enzyme?**

- A) Tyrosinase 
- B) Phenylalanine hydroxylase ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Homogentisate oxidase 
- D) Branched-chain alpha-keto acid dehydrogenase 

*Explanation:* Phenylketonuria (PKU) is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by a deficiency in phenylalanine hydroxylase (PAH). This enzyme converts phenylalanine to tyrosine. Without functional PAH, phenylalanine accumulates to toxic levels, leading to severe neurological damage if untreated.

---

**Q924: Which enzyme present in human saliva plays a crucial role in initiating carbohydrate digestion in the oral cavity?**

- A) Lingual lipase 
- B) Salivary amylase (ptyalin) ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Lysozyme 
- D) Salivary peroxidase 

*Explanation:* Salivary amylase, also known as ptyalin, is an enzyme secreted by the salivary glands that initiates the breakdown of complex carbohydrates (starch and glycogen) into smaller oligosaccharides and disaccharides in the mouth. Lingual lipase initiates lipid digestion, lysozyme has antibacterial properties, and salivary peroxidase is involved in antimicrobial defense.

---

**Q925: A patient with Xeroderma Pigmentosum (XP) develops multiple skin cancers due to extreme sensitivity to UV radiation. This genetic disorder is characterized by a defect in which major DNA repair pathway?**

- A) Mismatch repair 
- B) Base excision repair 
- C) Nucleotide excision repair ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Homologous recombination 

*Explanation:* Xeroderma Pigmentosum (XP) is caused by defects in the nucleotide excision repair (NER) pathway. NER is crucial for repairing bulky DNA lesions, such as pyrimidine dimers formed by UV radiation. Without functional NER, these lesions accumulate, leading to mutations and a high risk of skin cancers.

---

**Q926: Fructose-2,6-bisphosphate (F-2,6-BP) is a potent allosteric regulator that simultaneously activates phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1) and inhibits fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase. This dual action ensures what metabolic outcome?**

- A) Increased lipid synthesis and decreased protein breakdown. 
- B) Stimulation of both glycolysis and gluconeogenesis. 
- C) Coordinated regulation to favor glycolysis and inhibit gluconeogenesis. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Increased glycogen synthesis and decreased fatty acid oxidation. 

*Explanation:* F-2,6-BP is a key regulator that ensures reciprocal control of glycolysis and gluconeogenesis. By activating PFK-1 (a glycolytic enzyme) and inhibiting fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase (a gluconeogenic enzyme), it effectively switches the metabolic flux towards glucose breakdown (glycolysis) and away from glucose synthesis (gluconeogenesis), particularly in response to high glucose levels.

---

**Q927: Which of the following conditions would cause a RIGHT shift in the oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve, indicating a decreased affinity of hemoglobin for oxygen?**

- A) Increased pH (alkalosis) 
- B) Decreased temperature 
- C) Decreased 2,3-bisphosphoglycerate (2,3-BPG) levels 
- D) Increased partial pressure of CO2 ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* An increased partial pressure of CO2 (hypercapnia) leads to a right shift in the oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve (Bohr effect), meaning hemoglobin releases oxygen more readily to tissues. Other factors causing a right shift include decreased pH (acidosis), increased temperature, and increased 2,3-BPG levels. Conversely, the options A, B, and C would cause a left shift, indicating increased oxygen affinity.

---

**Q928: Elastin, a major component of elastic fibers, is characterized by its extensive cross-linking. The formation of these unique cross-links involves which of the following?**

- A) Hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues by Vitamin C-dependent enzymes. 
- B) Disulfide bonds between cysteine residues. 
- C) Desmosine and isodesmosine linkages formed from lysine residues. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Glycosylation of asparagine residues. 

*Explanation:* Elastin's elasticity is due to its unique desmosine and isodesmosine cross-links, which are formed extracellularly by the oxidative deamination of four lysine residues to allysine residues, followed by their condensation. This process is catalyzed by lysyl oxidase. Hydroxylation is crucial for collagen, disulfide bonds are common in many proteins but not the unique feature of elastin cross-links, and glycosylation is a different type of modification.

---

**Q929: A patient presents with severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to a deficiency in adenosine deaminase (ADA). This enzyme is crucial for the catabolism of adenosine. The accumulation of which substrate leads to lymphotoxicity in ADA deficiency?**

- A) Xanthine 
- B) Uric acid 
- C) Deoxyadenosine ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Inosine 

*Explanation:* Adenosine deaminase (ADA) catalyzes the deamination of adenosine and deoxyadenosine to inosine and deoxyinosine, respectively. In ADA deficiency, deoxyadenosine accumulates. High levels of deoxyadenosine are then phosphorylated to dATP, which inhibits ribonucleotide reductase, an enzyme essential for synthesizing other deoxyribonucleotides, leading to DNA synthesis arrest and lymphotoxicity.

---

**Q930: A 45-year-old patient presents with uncontrolled Type 1 diabetes. Which of the following metabolic alterations is most characteristic of this condition and directly contributes to ketoacidosis?**

- A) Increased activity of glucokinase in the liver. 
- B) Enhanced synthesis of fatty acids in adipose tissue. 
- C) Elevated breakdown of triacylglycerols in adipocytes. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Reduced gluconeogenesis in the liver. 

*Explanation:* In uncontrolled Type 1 diabetes, insulin deficiency leads to increased lipolysis in adipocytes, releasing large amounts of fatty acids. These fatty acids are then converted to ketone bodies in the liver, leading to ketoacidosis. The other options describe effects that are either incorrect or would mitigate ketoacidosis.

---

**Q931: Which of the following statements accurately describes the primary role of the pentose phosphate pathway (PPP) in a rapidly dividing cell, such as a cancer cell?**

- A) To produce ATP for cell division. 
- B) To generate NADH for oxidative phosphorylation. 
- C) To provide NADPH for reductive biosynthesis and ribose-5-phosphate for nucleotide synthesis. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) To synthesize glycogen for energy storage. 

*Explanation:* The pentose phosphate pathway's primary functions are to produce NADPH, essential for reductive biosynthesis (e.g., fatty acid and cholesterol synthesis) and detoxification of reactive oxygen species, and to produce ribose-5-phosphate, a precursor for nucleotide synthesis required for DNA and RNA in rapidly dividing cells. It does not primarily produce ATP or NADH for oxidative phosphorylation, nor is its main role glycogen synthesis.

---

**Q932: A patient is diagnosed with a deficiency in pyruvate dehydrogenase complex activity. Which of the following metabolic consequences would be most directly observed?**

- A) Accumulation of acetyl-CoA. 
- B) Increased conversion of pyruvate to lactate. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Decreased production of NADH in glycolysis. 
- D) Enhanced entry of fatty acids into the TCA cycle. 

*Explanation:* The pyruvate dehydrogenase complex converts pyruvate to acetyl-CoA. A deficiency would lead to a buildup of pyruvate, which would then be shunted towards lactate production via lactate dehydrogenase, especially under anaerobic conditions or when the TCA cycle is overwhelmed. This also limits acetyl-CoA formation, not accumulates it, and does not directly affect glycolytic NADH production or fatty acid entry into the TCA cycle.

---

**Q933: Which enzyme is the primary regulatory point for glycogenolysis in muscle tissue, responding to both hormonal signals and intracellular calcium levels?**

- A) Glycogen synthase. 
- B) Phosphofructokinase-1. 
- C) Glycogen phosphorylase. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Hexokinase. 

*Explanation:* Glycogen phosphorylase is the rate-limiting enzyme for glycogenolysis. In muscle, its activity is allosterically regulated by AMP (indicating low energy) and calcium (released during muscle contraction), and hormonally by epinephrine via phosphorylation. Glycogen synthase regulates glycogenesis, while phosphofructokinase-1 and hexokinase are involved in glycolysis.

---

**Q934: The synthesis of collagen involves several post-translational modifications. Which vitamin is absolutely essential for the hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues, a critical step for collagen fiber stability?**

- A) Vitamin K. 
- B) Vitamin B12. 
- C) Vitamin C (Ascorbic Acid). ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Vitamin D. 

*Explanation:* Vitamin C (ascorbic acid) is a crucial cofactor for prolyl hydroxylase and lysyl hydroxylase enzymes, which hydroxylate proline and lysine residues in procollagen. This hydroxylation is vital for the formation of stable cross-links within collagen fibrils. A deficiency leads to scurvy, characterized by fragile connective tissues.

---

**Q935: A patient presents with hyperammonemia following liver failure. Which biochemical pathway is primarily impaired, leading to the accumulation of ammonia?**

- A) Glycolysis. 
- B) TCA cycle. 
- C) Urea cycle. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Beta-oxidation. 

*Explanation:* The urea cycle is the primary pathway in the liver responsible for detoxifying ammonia by converting it into urea for excretion. Liver failure impairs this cycle, leading to the accumulation of toxic ammonia (hyperammonemia). The other pathways listed are not directly involved in ammonia detoxification.

---

**Q936: Which of the following statements regarding the structure and function of transfer RNA (tRNA) is correct?**

- A) tRNA molecules contain codons that specify amino acids. 
- B) tRNA molecules are synthesized in the nucleus and function in the cytoplasm. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) tRNA molecules carry ribosomal RNA to the ribosome. 
- D) Each tRNA molecule can bind to multiple types of amino acids. 

*Explanation:* tRNA molecules are synthesized in the nucleus by RNA polymerase III, then processed and exported to the cytoplasm where they function in protein synthesis. tRNA molecules contain anticodons, not codons, and each specific tRNA carries only one specific type of amino acid to the ribosome. They do not carry ribosomal RNA.

---

**Q937: In the context of enzymatic reactions, competitive inhibitors typically affect which kinetic parameter?**

- A) Decrease Vmax and decrease Km. 
- B) Increase Vmax and decrease Km. 
- C) Decrease Vmax and no change in Km. 
- D) No change in Vmax but increase Km. ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* Competitive inhibitors bind reversibly to the enzyme's active site, competing with the substrate. This effectively increases the apparent Km (more substrate is needed to reach half Vmax) but does not change the Vmax, as high substrate concentrations can outcompete the inhibitor. Non-competitive inhibitors typically decrease Vmax without changing Km.

---

**Q938: Which of the following statements best describes the role of insulin in carbohydrate metabolism?**

- A) It promotes glucose production by stimulating gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis. 
- B) It facilitates glucose uptake by all cells of the body, including neurons. 
- C) It increases glucose uptake into muscle and adipose tissue and promotes glycogen synthesis. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) It inhibits the synthesis of fatty acids and triacylglycerols. 

*Explanation:* Insulin is an anabolic hormone that promotes glucose uptake into insulin-sensitive tissues (muscle and adipose tissue) by stimulating GLUT4 translocation. It also promotes glycogen synthesis (glycogenesis) in the liver and muscle. It inhibits glucose production and promotes fatty acid synthesis, not inhibits it.

---

**Q939: The primary role of NADPH generated by the pentose phosphate pathway in red blood cells is to:**

- A) Provide energy for active transport. 
- B) Reduce methemoglobin to hemoglobin. 
- C) Maintain glutathione in its reduced form to protect against oxidative damage. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Synthesize fatty acids for membrane repair. 

*Explanation:* In red blood cells, NADPH is crucial for maintaining a high concentration of reduced glutathione, catalyzed by glutathione reductase. Reduced glutathione is then used by glutathione peroxidase to neutralize reactive oxygen species, protecting the cells from oxidative damage. Red blood cells lack mitochondria and do not synthesize fatty acids.

---

**Q940: Fluoride ions are known to inhibit glycolysis. Which enzyme is a primary target for fluoride inhibition?**

- A) Hexokinase. 
- B) Phosphofructokinase-1. 
- C) Enolase. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Pyruvate kinase. 

*Explanation:* Fluoride ions inhibit enolase, the enzyme that catalyzes the conversion of 2-phosphoglycerate to phosphoenolpyruvate in glycolysis. This inhibition is particularly relevant in dental contexts, as fluoride can inhibit bacterial glycolysis, reducing acid production. The other enzymes are not primary targets of fluoride inhibition.

---

**Q941: A patient with a genetic defect affecting the synthesis of elastin would primarily exhibit problems with which property of tissues?**

- A) Tensile strength and rigidity. 
- B) Compressive strength and hydration. 
- C) Elasticity and recoil. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Mineralization and hardness. 

*Explanation:* Elastin is a protein that provides tissues with elasticity, allowing them to stretch and recoil. Collagen provides tensile strength, while proteoglycans contribute to compressive strength and hydration. Defects in elastin synthesis would therefore primarily impair the elasticity and recoil properties of tissues.

---

**Q942: Which of the following molecules is the committed step regulator for de novo pyrimidine synthesis?**

- A) Carbamoyl phosphate synthetase I. 
- B) Ribonucleotide reductase. 
- C) Aspartate transcarbamoylase (ATCase). ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Dihydrofolate reductase. 

*Explanation:* Aspartate transcarbamoylase (ATCase) catalyzes the committed step in de novo pyrimidine synthesis, converting carbamoyl phosphate and aspartate into carbamoyl aspartate. Carbamoyl phosphate synthetase I is involved in the urea cycle, while ribonucleotide reductase and dihydrofolate reductase are involved in nucleotide interconversions and folate metabolism, respectively.

---

**Q943: During starvation, the brain can adapt to utilize which of the following as a primary energy source, besides glucose?**

- A) Fatty acids. 
- B) Amino acids. 
- C) Lactate. 
- D) Ketone bodies. ✅ **(CORRECT)**

*Explanation:* During prolonged starvation, the liver produces ketone bodies (acetoacetate and β-hydroxybutyrate) from fatty acid breakdown. While the brain typically relies on glucose, it can adapt to use ketone bodies as a significant energy source when glucose is scarce, thereby sparing muscle protein. The brain cannot directly utilize fatty acids for energy.

---

**Q944: Which of the following is the most direct consequence of a deficiency in Vitamin B1 (Thiamine)?**

- A) Impaired vision due to rhodopsin dysfunction. 
- B) Defective collagen cross-linking. 
- C) Reduced activity of pyruvate dehydrogenase and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Megaloblastic anemia due to impaired DNA synthesis. 

*Explanation:* Thiamine pyrophosphate (TPP), derived from Vitamin B1, is a crucial coenzyme for several key enzymes in carbohydrate metabolism, including pyruvate dehydrogenase and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase (part of the TCA cycle). A deficiency would significantly impair these pathways, leading to neurological and cardiac issues (Beriberi). The other options relate to deficiencies in other vitamins or metabolic issues.

---

**Q945: In the process of oxidative phosphorylation, the energy released from the electron transport chain is primarily used to:**

- A) Directly synthesize ATP from ADP and Pi. 
- B) Pump protons from the mitochondrial matrix to the intermembrane space. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Reduce oxygen to water without proton involvement. 
- D) Generate heat through uncoupling proteins. 

*Explanation:* The electron transport chain uses the energy from electron flow to pump protons from the mitochondrial matrix into the intermembrane space, creating an electrochemical proton gradient. This gradient, not direct ATP synthesis, is the energy source that drives ATP synthase to produce ATP from ADP and Pi. Uncoupling proteins dissipate this gradient as heat.

---

**Q946: The initial step of fatty acid synthesis, the carboxylation of acetyl-CoA to malonyl-CoA, is catalyzed by which key regulatory enzyme?**

- A) Fatty acid synthase. 
- B) Acetyl-CoA carboxylase. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Citrate synthase. 
- D) HMG-CoA reductase. 

*Explanation:* Acetyl-CoA carboxylase (ACC) catalyzes the irreversible carboxylation of acetyl-CoA to malonyl-CoA, which is the committed and rate-limiting step in fatty acid synthesis. This enzyme is highly regulated by hormones (insulin, glucagon) and allosteric effectors (citrate, palmitoyl-CoA). Fatty acid synthase is a multi-enzyme complex that extends the fatty acid chain, while HMG-CoA reductase is involved in cholesterol synthesis.

---

**Q947: Salivary amylase initiates carbohydrate digestion in the oral cavity. Its activity is optimal at a neutral pH but rapidly denatures in the acidic environment of the stomach. This enzyme primarily hydrolyzes which type of glycosidic bond?**

- A) Beta-1,4 glycosidic bonds in cellulose. 
- B) Alpha-1,6 glycosidic bonds in amylopectin. 
- C) Alpha-1,4 glycosidic bonds in starch and glycogen. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Beta-1,2 glycosidic bonds in sucrose. 

*Explanation:* Salivary amylase (alpha-amylase) specifically hydrolyzes alpha-1,4 glycosidic bonds found in starch (amylose and amylopectin) and glycogen, breaking them down into smaller polysaccharides, maltose, and other oligosaccharides. It cannot break beta-1,4 bonds (cellulose) or alpha-1,6 bonds, and sucrose is a disaccharide broken down by sucrase.

---

**Q948: A patient presents with symptoms of metabolic acidosis. Which of the following compensatory mechanisms would the body primarily employ to restore acid-base balance?**

- A) Increased renal reabsorption of bicarbonate ions. 
- B) Decreased respiratory rate to retain CO2. 
- C) Increased secretion of hydrogen ions by the kidneys. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Decreased production of ammonia by renal tubules. 

*Explanation:* In metabolic acidosis, the body attempts to excrete excess acid and retain bicarbonate. The kidneys respond by increasing the secretion of hydrogen ions (H+) into the urine and increasing the reabsorption of bicarbonate ions. Additionally, the kidneys increase ammonia production to buffer H+ ions. A decreased respiratory rate would worsen acidosis by retaining CO2.

---

**Q949: Which of the following hormones primarily promotes the synthesis of bone matrix proteins and the deposition of calcium in bone?**

- A) Parathyroid hormone (PTH). 
- B) Calcitriol (active Vitamin D). 
- C) Calcitonin. ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Cortisol. 

*Explanation:* Calcitonin, secreted by the thyroid gland, acts to lower blood calcium levels by inhibiting osteoclast activity and promoting the deposition of calcium into bone, including the synthesis of bone matrix proteins. PTH and calcitriol generally act to raise blood calcium, while cortisol has catabolic effects on bone at high concentrations.

---

**Q950: A patient presents with elevated lactate levels and symptoms of muscle fatigue, despite adequate oxygenation. Analysis reveals a defect in pyruvate dehydrogenase complex activity. Which of the following would *most likely* exacerbate this patient's condition?**

- A) Administration of thiamine (vitamin B1) 
- B) Increased intake of fatty acids 
- C) Consumption of a high-carbohydrate meal ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- D) Increased physical activity after a period of rest 

*Explanation:* A high-carbohydrate meal would significantly increase the flux through glycolysis, leading to an accumulation of pyruvate. Since the pyruvate dehydrogenase complex activity is deficient, this excess pyruvate would be shunted to lactate, worsening the patient's condition. Thiamine is a coenzyme for PDC, so its administration might help if it's a deficiency, but not exacerbate. Fatty acids and rest would reduce the need for PDC.

---

**Q951: A young child presents with brittle bones, blue sclera, and fragile teeth (dentinogenesis imperfecta). Genetic testing confirms a mutation in a gene encoding for Type I collagen. Which of the following post-translational modifications is *most critically* affected in the pathogenesis of such conditions?**

- A) Glycosylation of asparagine residues 
- B) Hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues ✅ **(CORRECT)**
- C) Acetylation of lysine residues 
- D) Phosphorylation of serine and threonine residues
